60.5 THE RENAME GLOSSARY, AND TWO LEADS IT LEAVES

  The 341 moves amount to a translation glossary, and the Slayer rename is
  systematic:

      characters/mummy_boss/*              -> characters/boss_mummy/*
      characters/morak_sword/*             -> characters/boss_morak_sword/*
      characters/skeleton_bandit/*         -> characters/skeleton_soldier/*
      gameobjects/story/morak_sword_shards -> gameobjects/story/boss_morak_sword_shards
      equipment/elf/wep_bow01_*            -> equipment/elf/weapon01_*
      characters/gator_brute/*             -> characters/boss_orox/*

  i.e. bosses were hoisted to a `boss_` prefix, and a few units were renamed
  outright.  Two bounded leads come out of it:

  (a) *Shared content, not renames.*  20 unresolved current resources are
      byte-identical to a pre-Slayer resource: 18 `animation` rows in
      `resource_packages/{morak, cave_boss, crypt_boss}` and 2 `material` rows in
      `resource_packages/all_effects`.  The old name is not the same shape, so these
      are the same content reused under a new name - a subject clue for resources
      that also appear in the live boss packages.  Re-running
      `tools/apply_renames.py --snap 2015-01` against the finished catalogue gives
      `rename examples 103  rename targets 1` and `history-translation candidates
      19,482  MATCHES 0`, i.e. the rename avenue holds no names we do not already
      have - and it also shows why the *translation* table must never be trusted
      blind: identical content under two unrelated names (say a shared impact effect
      used by two bosses) reads out as a bogus token translation like
      `wep -> cha`, so translations stay hypotheses, never names.

60.6 THE TREANT: THE ONE ENEMY THAT LEAVES NOTHING BUT A STRING

  `treant` appears in exactly one place - the 2015-08 depot strings - and only as
  `treant_branch_trap` (with `_01`/`_02`/`_diff`/`_norm`/`_spec`/`_var01` siblings and
  the localization key `loc_enemy_treant_branch_trap`).  It is in neither 2014-10-10
  nor 2015-01-21, it is not in the game binary's strings, and no spelling of it - 349
  precise candidates across the plausible trap/prop/character/effects directories -
  hashes to any row of the shipped build, whether already named or still unresolved.
  The asset name behind the cut enemy is likely
  `characters/ent_treespirit/ent_treespirit`, which is also a dangling string with no
  row.  So a tree enemy with one ability ("branch trap") was cut, and what survives is
  *text*, not a resource: `treant_branch_trap` does occur in `bundle_strings.txt`,
  i.e. inside a shipped bundle, but nothing in the build is *named* it.

  The useful by-product is the vocabulary source that exposed it.  `loc_enemy_*` is a
  localization namespace for enemy abilities: 224 keys, 32 distinct stems, 16 enemy
  unit stems.  Fifteen of the sixteen are live - `mummy`, `skeleton`, `cultist`,
  `demon`, `spider`, `grunt`, `orc`, `beast`, `ghost`, `lake`, `lich`, `morak`,
  `necromancer`, `spawner`, `tyrfing` - and **`treant` is the only one that is not**,
  which is exactly what makes a cut unit look recoverable.  Those 32 stems were also
  tried as resource names in every plausible ability/trap directory: 0 hits.  The same
  namespace independently confirms 60.2 from the other direction:
  `loc_enemy_beast_of_orox` is the game's own user-facing name for that unit, so the
  entity really was billed as the Beast of Orox.

60.7 THE DEMON IMP IS `demon_ranged`; THE TREANT'S TRAP IS A BORROWED `enemy_type`

  The imp strings are identical in all three harvests - `demon_imp` plus
  `_01`/`_02`/`_diff`/`_norm`/`_spec`/`_var01` (the texture-set shape) and the loc key
  `loc_enemy_demon_imp` - and the new `check_build_liveness.py` settles it: not one
  spelling is a live resource in 2014-10-10, 2015-01-21 or 2015-08-11, and none has a
  row in the shipped catalogue.  Two controls ran in the same batch, so the verdict is
  trustworthy: `characters/gator_brute/gator_brute` reads LIVE in 2014 and 2015-01 and
  gone in 2015-08 (the rename we already proved), and `characters/lizard_base` reads
  absent, absent, LIVE - a name added *by* the shipped build.

  The name has not left the shipped build, though: it is in **live Lua**.  A
  `--grep demon_imp|treant` pass over every resource body (173,664 distinct strings)
  finds it in exactly three entries, all `lua`, all already named:

      characters/demon_ranged/demon_ranged            contains loc_enemy_demon_imp, demon_imp
      characters/relic_gargoyle/relic_gargoyle        contains loc_enemy_demon_imp, demon_imp
      characters/cultist_armor/cultist_armor_corpse   contains treant_branch_trap

  and the context is a unit-definition table - the fields the lua gives the unit:

      name        = loc_enemy_demon_imp
      enemy_type  = demon_imp

  So the enemy the game calls *Demon Imp* is the unit whose assets are named
  `demon_ranged`.  The same readout maps the whole demon family, because that lua
  bundle declares `enemy_type` for 53 units:

      characters/demon_ranged/demon_ranged            enemy_type = demon_imp
      characters/demon_melee/demon_melee              enemy_type = demon_fiend
      characters/demon_heavy/demon_heavy              enemy_type = demon_horror
      characters/demon_overlord_mini/...              enemy_type = demon_horror
      characters/relic_gargoyle/relic_gargoyle        enemy_type = demon_imp
      characters/boss_orox/boss_orox                  enemy_type = beast_of_orox

  i.e. the three dead loc names map onto the three live demon units (imp = ranged,
  fiend = melee, horror = heavy), which is why no spelling of `demon_imp`,
  `demon_fiend` or `demon_horror` is a resource: they were *gameplay* names, never
  asset names.  `boss_orox` declaring `beast_of_orox` is 60.2 again, from the code
  side.

  `treant_branch_trap` is the other kind of survivor: it is not a display name for its
  own unit but an `enemy_type` *borrowed* by `cultist_armor_corpse`, so the trap
  archetype outlived the treant.  `enemy_type` is an archetype id and not a rename
  record - `characters/cultist_armor/cultist_armor` and `characters/living_statue/...`
  both declare `orc_juggernaut`, and `characters/boss_morak_sword/...` declares
  `tyrfing` - so it maps a unit to a *behaviour* and only sometimes coincides with the
  unit's own name.

  Practical value: this is a second, independent channel for the "cut, or merely
  unnamed?" question, and it produced the whole `asset -> enemy_type` map for 53
  units.  It adds no *names* - the strings inside those bodies hash to no unresolved
  target, which `scan_bundle_strings.py` reports as `DIRECT HITS: 0` - but it says
  which live asset is which in-game enemy, which is exactly what the referrer hunts of
  section 60.5 could not settle.

60.8 THE OLD SPAWNERS: COLOUR NAMES, AND A LIVE TABLE THAT STILL POINTS AT THEM

  Spawners were named by colour and by group, and the shape survives in the shipped
  strings as one subdirectory per group:

      gameobjects/spawners/spawner_swamp_red/spawner_swamp_red_t1        (+ _01 _02 _diff _norm _spec _var01)
      gameobjects/spawners/spawner_swamp_red/spawner_swamp_red_t2        (+ the same set)
      gameobjects/spawners/spawner_swamp_red/spawner_swamp_red_t3        (+ the same set)
      gameobjects/spawners/spawner_swamp_swamp/spawner_swamp_swamp_t1..t3
      gameobjects/spawners/spawner_swamp_corruption/spawner_swamp_corruption_t1..t3
      gameobjects/gold/goldspawner                                       (+ the same set)

  Exactly one of those is a live resource: `gameobjects/gold/goldspawner`, named since
  2014-10-10 - the gold spawner.  Every swamp/red name has no row in any of the three
  builds, and no other colour appears: there is no blue/green/black spawner, and
  `crown` never occurs in a spawner name in any build (the crown strings are treasure
  and HUD - `gameobjects/treasures/crown/crown`, `crown_bonus`,
  `add_crown_knocked_off`).

  What makes these names interesting is where they come from.  `lua/generated/
  spawners_unit_path` is a live, already-named Lua resource and its body *is* the
  spawner manifest: 23 unit paths.  Eighteen resolve - `spawner`, `spawner_beam`,
  `spawner_caves_grunt`, `spawner_caves_orc`, `spawner_crypt_mummy(_t1)`,
  `spawner_crypt_skeleton(_t1)`, `spawner_exploding`, `spawner_freeze_nova`,
  `spawner_invisible`, `spawner_lava_cultist`, `spawner_lava_daemon`,
  `spawner_mortar`, `spawner_poison_launcher`, `spawner_trap`, `spawner_twister`,
  `goldspawner` - and five do not:

      gameobjects/spawners/spawner_swamp_red/spawner_swamp_red_t1   no row in any build
      gameobjects/spawners/spawner_swamp_red/spawner_swamp_red_t2   no row in any build
      gameobjects/spawners/spawner_teleport                         no row in any build
      gameobjects/spawners/spawner_tutorial                         no row in any build

  So the shipped build's own generated spawner table still points at spawners cut
  before the earliest snapshot we hold, complete with their variant sets
  (`spawner_teleport` and `spawner_tutorial` each have seven spellings in the
  strings).  Same pattern as `demon_imp` (60.7) and `treant_branch_trap` (60.6): the
  name is kept alive by live code or a live table, which is exactly what makes a cut
  thing look recoverable, and the hash test is what settles it.

  No names were added - all 447 `gameobjects/spawners/*` and `gameobjects/gold/*`
  strings were hashed and not one is an unresolved row - but
  `lua/generated/spawners_unit_path` is worth remembering as a *witness list*: it is
  the one place where a cut spawner's exact path is still spelled out in full, so any
  future spawner work should start from it.

60.9 THE PRE-SLAYER BUILD IS A DIFFERENT GAME'S WORTH OF CONTENT, AND IT IS ALL ON DISK

  The rename hunt (60.2) turned up something bigger than renames.
  `hunt_build_diff.py --old 2014 --new 2015-08` reports, across the 47 bundles the two
  snapshots share:

      old_only             2,619   existed in 2014-10-10, gone from the shipped build
      new_only             4,704   added by the shipped build
      content_moved          339   same bytes, different name (a rename)
      same_hash_changed    4,298   SAME NAME, DIFFERENT BYTES - reworked in place
                                   (3,750 of them are resources whose names we hold)

  `--report-changed`, added for this, lists the reworked ones.  Narrowed to the cast,
  the picture is unmistakable: of 130 unit candidates (26 units x body, `_diff`,
  `_norm`, `_spec`, idle), 37 exist in 2014 and 57 in the shipped build; of those,
  10 are byte-identical, 27 changed and 20 are new.  The ones whose *body and
  animation* changed are the models:

      characters/demon_heavy/demon_heavy        changed   (its idle and _diff changed too)
      characters/demon_ranged/demon_ranged      changed   (idle and _diff changed too)
      characters/demon_melee/demon_melee        changed
      characters/mummy_priest/mummy_priest      changed
      characters/mummy_risen/mummy_risen        changed   (in 2014 it was byte-identical to mummy_giant)
      characters/orc_juggernaut/orc_juggernaut  changed
      characters/cultist_novice/cultist_novice  changed   (cultist_novice_diff is byte-identical)
      characters/cultist_sorcerer/...           changed
      characters/lich/lich                      changed   (lich_diff byte-identical)
      characters/lake_dweller/...               changed
      characters/grunt_shaman/...               changed
      characters/ghost/ghost                    changed   (ghost_diff and ghost_norm byte-identical)

  Two things follow.  The PreSlayer units are **not** the shipped units under an older
  name: the same names carry different geometry, and the diffuse map was often kept
  while the mesh and animation were redone - which is exactly what a re-model looks
  like.  And `demon_overlord_mini` is the only overlord in any build (shipped, and
  named); `characters/demon_overlord/*` and `characters/overlord/overlord` have no row
  anywhere, and "overlord" appears in neither the 2014 nor the 2015-01 strings - so the
  full-size overlord is older than every snapshot we hold, and would have to come from
  an earlier depot manifest.

  Which is the real point: the old content is still on disk.  We hold
  `Gauntlet-PreSlayer-2014-10-10` (5,489 live resource hashes), `...-2015-01-21`
  (6,224) and `...-2015-08-11` (7,804) alongside the shipped install, so the pre-Slayer
  models, textures and animations are all readable, and the depot harvests still carry
  names from builds we do not have (that is where `lizard_archer`, `treant_branch_trap`
  and `demon_imp` come from), so older manifests remain reachable.

  That makes *restoring* the old content possible.  The toolchain already knows how to
  build and inject custom packages - §16 documents the custom unit/material/texture
  chain that loads in-game - so an old resource could be repacked under its own name
  and loaded, or referenced from a custom unit, and the PreSlayer look would come back:
  the original demon models, the lizard units, `gator_brute` as it looked before it
  became Orox, the treant, and the `spawner_swamp_*` family.  That is a
  content-restoration project rather than a name-recovery one, and it is **out of scope
  here**; it is written down so the lead is not lost.  Same licensing caution as §58.4 -
  reading and modding locally is one thing, redistributing shipped assets is not.

  Starting points if it is ever picked up: the three snapshot directories; this tool
  with `--report-changed` for the re-modelled resources and `--dump-renames` for the
  renamed ones; `check_build_liveness.py` to say which build holds any given name; and
  the `_names.txt` harvests for names whose build we no longer have at all.

60.10 "CLEAVE": THE ANIMATIONS AND ABILITIES ARE NOT RESOURCES AT ALL

  Looking for cleave animations and abilities turns out to be a good test of the whole
  method, because almost none of them exist as resources.  We hold exactly one
  cleave-named resource in the shipped build, and it is the HUD icon:

      hud/action_icons/warrior_action_demoncleave    texture, LIVE only in 2015-08, named

  Everything else cleave is a *value inside a live resource*.  The animations are
  animation **event names**, registered in `lua/generated/animation_events` - a live,
  named Lua resource, 475 strings, 93 of them `attack_*` events:

      attack_demoncleave_execute
      attack_demoncleave_windup, attack_demoncleave_windup_done
      attack_demonslash, attack_slash, attack_slash_windup, attack_fast_slash, ...

  Those events fire on animations we already hold - the warrior's own
  `characters/avatar_warrior/animations/axe_*` - so there is no animation resource to
  find and no hash to match.  ~5,000 candidate spellings were tried anyway
  (weapon-prefixed, per-unit, per-directory, with the usual `_01`/`_02`/`_diff`/`_norm`/
  `_spec`/`_var01` suffix sets): **0 hits**.

  The abilities are the same story.  Cleave is a table inside the *weapon* Lua, not a
  standalone ability resource:

      equipment/warrior/weapon04   demon cleave - ui_info warrior_action_demoncleave,
                                   event_name attack_demoncleave_*, damage_type cleave,
                                   backup_unit_path special_demoncleave_projectile
      equipment/warrior/weapon11   plain cleave - ui_info warrior_action_cleave

  Both weapons are live and named, as are the Lua scripts that drive them.  Two names
  inside them are dangling, with no row in any build: `warrior_action_cleave` (the plain
  cleave's icon, referenced by weapon11) and `special_demoncleave_projectile` (absent
  from the unit-path registries too).  So the icon the *plain* cleave asks for never
  shipped - only the demon-cleavage icon did.

  The old naming is visible in the strings as `warrior_axe_cleave_heavy` and
  `loc_control_warrior_cleave_attack` (a control-binding localization key), present in
  all three builds, plus the 2014/2015-01 family `anim_attack_cleave`,
  `cleave_collision_origin`, `cleave_collision_half_extents` and `cleave_exit` (state
  fields of the old ability), and `hit_cleave_armor`/`_bone`/`_flesh`/`_ethereal`/`_hero`
  (hit-react and damage-type values).  None of them is a resource in any build.

  The general lesson is worth keeping.  `lua/generated/*` is a family of **registries** -
  fourteen of them, including `animation_events`, `flow_events`, `unit_paths`,
  `unit_paths_short_lookup`, `spawners_unit_path`, `characters_unit_path`,
  `network_messages`, `network_object_types` and `units_with_settings` - and they spell
  names out in full.  A name that lives in one of those registries but hashes to no row
  was never a resource name: it is an event, a flow, a field or a path alias.  That is a
  third class of false lead alongside cut content (60.6-60.9) and harvest-only strings
  (60.4), and this is the check that tells the three apart.

60.11 THE 57 UNRESOLVED `unit` ROWS: IDENTIFIED, BUT NOT NAMEABLE BY CONVENTION

  The level-`unit` cluster was the one hole that looked like characters, so it got a
  full investigation.  There are 70 such rows and 57 distinct hashes, all inside five
  level-module packages - `modules/d01_caves/resources` (24), `d01_lava` (18),
  `d01_crypt` (6), `menu` (5), `d01_hub_room` (4) - and their bodies are the meshes
  themselves, from 903 bytes to 3.63 MB.  What the investigation established:

  1. *They are not copies of anything named.*  Walking every bundle and comparing
     content hashes against all 57 target bodies gives **zero matches**, named or
     unnamed.  So they are unique level-local assets: unlike the level's props, which
     are copies of global `props/...` assets, these exist once.

  2. *Eleven of them name their own subject through what they reference.*  An aligned
     u64 scan of each body against our 6,709 named resources mostly comes up empty
     (these units reference their materials by thin hash, not by full name), but it
     did find the ones below.  That is enough to say what they are, even where the
     name is out of reach:

         94c4e8676a6b9668  876 B   lavarock_floor_01_norm, lavarock_rock_01_norm,
                                   lavarock_wall_01_norm, lavarock_floor_border_01_norm,
                                   lava_tile_blend_mask, props/d01_lava/lava_geyser_norm
                                   -> a multi-tile terrain stamp blending four lava rocks
          db79169660ed9629  324 B   lavastruct_stairs_norm, lavastruct_borders_01_norm
         135ab7fc557b0e7b 23 KB    blocks/d01_caves/caverock_set
         eda3b17372d851e4 16 KB    blocks/d01_caves/caverock_set
        5171038d70d672d0 10 KB    blocks/d01_caves/caverock_set
         5b6437107c269db5  8 KB    blocks/d01_caves/swamp_set
         e55b8a5ce3a58d36  7 KB    blocks/d01_caves/swamp_set
         05b95a956d2b2f06 650 KB   blocks/d01_lava/lavastruct_set
         1b53f5adcfff1461 647 KB   blocks/d01_lava/lavastruct_set
         93f1d4141e624ab2 492 B    props/d01_lava/lava_tile_blend_mask   (menu module)
         69d0416658f5f934 376 B    blocks/d01_caves/caves_water_01_norm
                                   (crypt module)

     So the small ones are terrain tiles/stamps and the large ones are the
     corresponding tile meshes; the rest (the 46 with no named reference) are props
     whose materials are referenced only by thin hash.

  3. *Every convention-based route is closed.*  The evidence, in the order it was
     tried: the bodies contain no name strings (binary mesh data, and
     `scan_bundle_strings.py` reports `DIRECT HITS: 0` build-wide); content identity
     matches nothing; the nearest-size named neighbours are props and blocks from
     *every* level rather than the unit's own (the closest coincidence anywhere is
     78,099 bytes against `blocks/d01_caves/walls/caverock_wall_4c` - same size,
     different bytes); sibling and single/double-axis mutation of all 713 named
     `props/d01_*` and `blocks/d01_*` seeds, including directory moves between flat
     and `floors`/`walls`/`borders`/`rocks`/`pillars`/`stairs`/`railings`, ran
     532,500 candidates for nothing; and the module namespace is the wrong *type* -
     `modules/.../prefabs/**`, `quilts/**` (497 names), `dungeon_tiles/**`, `rooms`,
     `colosseum` and `arena` are all `level` resources, not `unit`, so the prefab
     naming convention (`prefab_<env>_<descriptors>[_NN|_Nc]`, discovered while
     looking) cannot name a unit.  Pattern sweeps over that prefab space - 4.4e10
     candidates two-word, 2.84e10 three-word - returned zero as well.

  4. *And the generic sweeps had already been here.*  The positional token tiers
     (pos4-pos7, postail4-postail7, widetail, postail5 still running) cover exactly
     this `dir%a_%b_%c_%d` space for props/blocks/gameobjects, and `big_mutate`
     mutates 49,156 seeds against an 80,000-token vocabulary.  Both are `+0` here.

  5. *The purpose-built twin-naming tool ran too, and found twins but no names.*
     `generators/effect_transplant.py` (nearest known body of the same type by byte
     similarity, then substitute one or two tokens from the family vocabulary,
     accepting a candidate only if the hash matches) was run for `--type unit`
     across all five bundles.  It found real twins for 16 of the 57 - the closest
     being `b24d44becaece1d5` against `blocks/d01_caves/walls/caverock_wall_4c` at
     100 % similarity and the same 78,099 bytes, then
     `blocks/d01_caves/floors/cavestruct_floor_4c_4c` (67.6 %),
     `props/d01_common/fog_plane` (62.5 %) and `blocks/common/invisible_wall_2c`
     (46.3 %) - but named none of them.  A follow-up sweep of 2,724 candidates
     centred on those twins (suffix and variant sets, rock/part/size token swaps,
     and level/directory moves) was empty as well.  A body that is near-identical
     to a named asset under a name that is *not* a variant of it is precisely what
     an authored, per-level copy looks like.

  Conclusion: these 57 are **authored, level-local unit names whose text exists
  nowhere in the shipped build and which no naming convention reaches** - the same
  kind of wall as the shader leaves (§17.9), but for 57 resources instead of 407.
  They are identified in substance (terrain tiles, tile meshes, and the props that
  use the terrain materials) without being nameable, and no sweep - GPU tiers,
  mutate passes, prefab patterns, or the generator in
  `hashcracker/generators/level_units.py` - moves them.  Recorded as closed rather
  than pending, so no future pass spends GPU on them again.

  Side result worth keeping: the module namespace has its own naming grammar -
  `modules/d01_<env>/prefabs/<category>/prefab_<env>_<descriptors>[_<NN>|_<Nc>]`,
  `modules/d01_<env>/quilts/<category>/<env>_quilt_<size>__<grid-code>__<NN>[_<descriptor>]`,
  `modules/d01_<env>/dungeon_tiles/<rock>_<dims>_<quad>_<quad>_<quad>_<quad>_<NN>[_corridor]`,
  plus `rooms`, `colosseum` and `arena` - all `level`-typed.

60.12 THE HUB ROOM: REMOVED, WITH ITS REFERENCES STILL IN THE LUA

  The hub room is the cleanest example of a *removed area* in the whole project, and
  the harvest-per-build comparison shows it precisely.  Extracting every hub-room path
  from each build's `_names.txt` gives:

      2014-10-10   106 paths      2015-01-21   106 paths      2015-08-11    42 paths

  and of the 106 distinct paths across all three builds, **not one has a row in the
  shipped build** - they are all gone, including `blocks/d01_hub_room/entrances/
  hub_level_doorway_01*`, `modules/d01_hub_room/hub_room`, `..._hub_room_v2`,
  `..._hub_room_v3`, `..._hub_room_old_environments`, `props/d01_hub_room/
  merchant_placeholder*` and the `tutorial_room` siblings.  What the shipped build
  keeps of that area is the module skeleton and the shop furniture:

      modules/d01_hub_room/shading_environment, tutorial_room,
                          tutorial_room_sandplane, tutorial_room_walls,
                          hub_room_teleport
      shaders/hubroom_logo_pow
      props/d01_hub_room/gauntlet_logo_outer, merchant_barrel, merchant_box,
                         merchant_rug
      plus 4 unit resources still unresolved: e1fbae1cb7a59595 (28,584 B),
           cff5c6316b30bf53 (112,539 B), 2e5618907cc69809 (173,857 B),
           57a38789f2c4baa5 (793,565 B)

  The Lua still points at the removed area, which is what makes it reachable at all:
  `lua/generated/unit_paths_short_lookup` (the live short-name -> path registry) maps
  `merchant_barrel`, `merchant_box` and `gauntlet_logo_outer` to their
  `props/d01_hub_room/...` paths, and `lua/generated/flow_events` still lists
  `hub_room_intro_started`, `hub_room_intro_stopped`, `hubroom_sequence`,
  `hub_entering_vo_done` and `barrel_died` - i.e. the hub-room intro sequence and the
  destructible barrel are still events in the shipped build even though the room is
  not.  (The "crate" in that memory is `merchant_box`; "crate" itself appears nowhere
  in the shipped Lua.)

  Naming the four leftovers failed the same way as the rest of the cluster: a
  45,260-candidate hub/merchant sweep (ten hub directories x the merchant, crate,
  barrel, logo, teleport and tutorial vocabulary, with suffix sets and two-word
  combinations) returned nothing, all 106 old paths have no row, and these bodies
  have no twin at all - the twin search reports 0 % similarity to every known
  resource, against 100 % for some of the dungeon-tile units in 60.11.  They are
  unique hub-room meshes whose names appear in no shipped text.

  Method worth reusing: comparing a name's *presence per build* from the harvests
  against its *status in the shipped catalogue* is the cheapest way to prove what an
  update removed, and the hub room is the demo - 106 paths in, zero rows out.

60.13 WHERE NAMES ARE NOT: THE SOURCE TREE, THE STREAMS, THE CONFIGS, THE DUMP

  The shipped install carries several things we had never mined, so each was checked
  for names.  All are dead ends, and recording that saves re-checking:

  * `Gauntlet-Source-DO_NOT_EDIT/` - 927 shipped **Lua source files** with real
    filenames (lua/ 363, foundation/ 173, gameobjects/ 123, gui/ 108, characters/ 108,
    equipment/ 44, ability_units/ 5, props/ 3).  Every one of the 927 paths is already
    a *named* resource (0 wins, 0 unknown), and the file contents yield 1,870
    path-like strings that add nothing.  The tree is the same name set we already
    have, not a bigger one.
  * `contents/*.stream` (11 files, 1-33 MB) - compressed payloads; the only readable
    markers are embedded `OggS` audio.  No headers to harvest.
  * `contents/settings*.ini`, `test.ini`, `bundle_database.data`, `ClientRegistry.blob`
    - zero path-like strings between them.
  * `binaries/bitsquid_win32_release_*_crash_2015_5_21*.mdmp` - a shipped engine crash
    dump, 31 KB of stripped stack data, no path-like strings.
  * `binaries/*.dll|exe` (`bin_strings.txt`, already mined) - contains the engine's own
    scripting API names (`load_bank`, `soundbank`, `timpani_bank`, `timpani_world`,
    `trigger_timpani_event`, `update_banks`) but no asset names.  That is where our
    `timpani_bank` *type* comes from.
  * `shader_library_group` bodies (47 resources, all named) - binary; they do not
    enumerate their members' flags, so the 165 unresolved shader chains cannot be
    completed from them.

  Combined with §57.1 (the shipped database holds no names), §58.1 (every readable
  artifact class is mined) and the build-wide `DIRECT HITS: 0` from
  `scan_bundle_strings.py`, this settles it: **there is no unmined header, table or
  manifest left in the shipped build that carries resource names.**  The remaining
  1,096 have to come from grammar + vocabulary, or not at all.

  Where the 1,096 actually are, counting each hash once by dominant type (the
  per-type table in section 59.2 counts a hash under every type it carries, so it
  sums higher than 1,096):

      352  texture        atlas and map-sibling rules already mined per bundle
      330  animation      per-unit clip grammar; owners bind via state machines
                          (`extract_anim_refs.py`), verb+number grammar mined
      203  shader_library + shader   the `technique:[flags]` wall - a *flag vocabulary*
                          gap rather than a brute-force gap, since
                          `generators/shader_chains.py` can mix an outside flag
                          vocabulary (`--extra-flags`); the lever is to mine flags out
                          of the 1,131 shader rows we already name
      111  material + particles   per-family generators saturated
       52  unit           authored, proven unreachable (60.11)
       37  timpani_bank   rigid `audio/enemy_<unit>` / `audio/heroes_<hero>` grammar,
                          but a 2,411-candidate sweep missed - it needs its
                          per-package context read rather than more guessing
       10  level          tiles/quilts, closed with 60.11
        1  flow

60.14 THE REACHABILITY AUDIT, AND THE SHADER FLAG SOURCE

  Two things were missing from the picture: nobody had measured *whether* an
  unresolved name is reachable, and the shader chains had never been given the flag
  vocabulary the renderer itself declares.  Both are now done.

  **Reachability** (`reachability_audit.py` -> `bundle_manifest/09_reachability.tsv`).
  For every resource it walks all bundle bodies, collects the 8-byte-aligned values
  that are catalogue hashes (Stingray references resources by hash), and counts who
  references whom.  Result over the 1,096 unresolved:

      1,044  referenced by at least one NAMED resource
         52  referenced by nothing named at all

      animation  330/0   shader_library 165/0   unit 57/0   texture 316/36
      particles   83/8   shader 36/2            timpani 34/3
      material    14/1   level 8/2              flow 1/0     (reachable / orphaned)

  So reachability is not the bottleneck - 95 % of the remainder is pointed at by
  something we can read.  The bottleneck is the *mapping* from a referrer's name to
  the referent's name, which for authored content does not exist (60.11).  The 52
  orphans are the ones where even the subject is unknown; the most-referenced
  unresolved resources are the level-unit cluster (the crypt quilt that places
  `1f01e19947211248` 99 times) and effects textures reached by `effects/*hit*` names
  (349 references into one 43,836-byte texture).

  **The shader flag source.**  The `render_config` resources are the renderer's own
  manifest, and they list both the shader libraries and - crucially - the
  `shader_pass_flags` the engine can set:

      forward_lighting_disabled, forward_lighting_one_light,
      forward_lighting_two_lights, forward_lighting_three_lights,
      forward_lighting_four_lights, dev_wireframe
      (plus the texture-channel flags we already knew: diffuse_map, normal_map,
       material_map, height_map, self_illumination_map, reflection_map)

  Six of those are **pass** flags and none of them appears in any chain we have
  already named, which is exactly why `generators/shader_chains.py` could not finish
  the job: it can only combine flags the technique was *seen* with.  Feeding the pass
  flags into the pool is the fix, and `hunt_shader_subsets.py` does that alongside a
  size filter - within a technique, body size predicts the flag count (`base`:
  1 flag 68.6 KB ... 9 flags 131.4 KB, 423 samples), and each technique has its own
  size band, so an unresolved shader's size says which technique it is and roughly
  how many flags it carries.

  A self-test of the enumeration on held-out *known* chains is part of the story: with
  the full vocabulary it recovers what it should, but it misses chains whose flags are
  rare in the corpus, which is why the pass-flag list above matters more than any
  amount of extra subset enumeration.

  The audit also produces the **master list**, `bundle_manifest/10_unresolved_master.tsv`
  - one row per unresolved resource with an explicit status:

      status        count   types
      reachable     1,044   animation 330, texture 316, shader_library 165, particles 83,
                            unit 57, shader 36, timpani_bank 34, material 14, level 8, flow 1
      UNREACHABLE      52   texture 36, particles 8, timpani_bank 3, level 2, shader 2,
                            material 1     (50 "nothing references it", 2 "no named
                            referrer" - members of an unresolved-only cluster)

  "UNREACHABLE" means no *named* resource points at it, so its name cannot be derived by
  inference - not that it is hopeless: it stays in the sweep targets
  (`data/vocab/unresolved_targets.txt` is built from the catalogue, never from this
  file), so the engines keep guessing it.  The marking exists so an inference pass and
  a brute-force pass can be told apart at a glance, and so the progress report can say
  how much of the remainder is inference-reachable at all.

  Result of the pass-flag shader sweep, run the way the project normally runs these -
  generate a candidate list, hash it on the local GPU (`tools/hc.sh crack --gpu
  --targets data/vocab/targets_shader_library.txt --candidates F`, 165 shader targets):

      top-14 pool + 13 pass/texture flags   40,655,888 candidates   0 hits   (0.3 s)
      top-20 pool + same flags, de-duplicated   19,093,745 candidates   0 hits   (0.2 s)

  and then the same idea inside the cracker itself, which is the better shape for this:
  `mutate` now understands chains.  Given the 366 chains we have named, the 39
  techniques among them, and a 109-flag vocabulary (every flag seen in a chain plus the
  render_config pass flags), it drops a flag, replaces one or two, adds N, or swaps the
  technique - and it **always emits the chain with its flags sorted**, because that is
  the only order the engine ever writes, so no permutation is wasted.  Runs:

      --chain-depth 2     0 hits   (0.2 s)
      --chain-depth 3     0 hits   (1.7 s)
      --chain-depth 4     0 hits  (21.0 s)

  So the chains are *not* sorted subsets of the flags we can name, even with the pass
  flags, and they are not within four flags of any chain we already have.  That is a
  real answer rather than a shrug: the remaining 165 use flags or techniques that
  appear nowhere in our vocabulary, which is why every enumeration route keeps
  returning zero.  Two things bound how far enumeration can go from here: the flag pool
  cannot simply be widened (the pattern space over a full 60-flag vocabulary at 6-9
  flags is 1e13-1e18 candidates, which is why the size model and the frequency ranking
  exist), and a self-test on held-out known chains shows the enumeration only recovers
  what its vocabulary covers.  For these 165 the remaining source is not in any shipped
  text - it is what the engine actually binds at runtime, i.e. a GPU frame capture
  (RenderDoc) of the game.

  (b) *The 70 unresolved `unit` rows are the character-shaped hole.*  They sit in
      the level modules - `modules/d01_caves/resources` (29), `d01_lava` (23),
      `menu` (7), `d01_crypt` (6), `d01_hub_room` (5) - and several hashes repeat
      across modules (`1f01e19947211248` in both crypt and menu), the signature of
      one definition placed in more than one level.  Named `unit` rows in those same
      modules are level-placed props, blocks, spawners, traps and character
      placements, so these are most likely run-of-the-level geometry - but they are
      not all named, and their bodies are the one place left where the shipped build
      may still carry a name in plain text.  Content identity against the pre-Slayer
      builds is the second way in.  Both are worth a pass before the next 200-name
      tier.

60.15 THE NAME->HASH FUNCTION, FOUND BY DISASSEMBLY (AND WHY THE RUNTIME SCAN COULD NOT)

  The runtime capture project (GauntletModLoader) set out to locate the engine's
  name->hash function from *inside* the process: scan the loaded game image for the
  MurmurHash64A multiplier (0x5bd1e995 / 0xc6a4a793), then look for the 47/33-bit
  shifts nearby.  Three scan revisions (v1 byte pair, v2 constant plus the code that
  references it, v3 a wider +/-0x400 window scoring shr/shrd/shld) all failed the
  same way:

      murmur constant at 0 site(s), 0 code reference(s) to it
      (immediate-form fallback: 4 candidate(s)) - every candidate scored 2, refused

  The scan was not wrong about the neighbourhood, only about its *shape*.  Reading
  the executable with a disassembler instead (`objdump -D -M intel gauntlet.exe`)
  settles it in one shot: the two halves of the 64-bit constant are never adjacent.

      4061a4:  69 c0 95 e9 d1 5b    imul eax,eax,0x5bd1e995
      4061aa:  69 c9 95 e9 d1 5b    imul ecx,ecx,0x5bd1e995
      4061b7:  69 d2 95 e9 d1 5b    imul edx,edx,0x5bd1e995
      4061e6:  69 c0 95 e9 d1 5b    imul eax,eax,0x5bd1e995
      4061ec:  8b c8 / c1 e9 0d     mov ecx,eax / shr ecx,0xd
      4061f3:  69 c9 95 e9 d1 5b    imul ecx,ecx,0x5bd1e995
      4061fb:  c1 e8 0f             shr  eax,0xf
      406202:  c3                   ret

      406210:  53 55 56 57          push ebx / ebp / esi / edi   <- 64-bit core entry
      406214:  8b 7c 24 18          mov  edi,[esp+0x18]          <- 2nd stack argument
      406218:  68 93 a7 a4 c6       push 0xc6a4a793              <- high half of M
      406220:  68 95 e9 d1 5b       push 0x5bd1e995              <- low half of M
      406227:  e8 a4 9e 1f 00       call 0x6000d0                <- 64x64 multiply helper

  A single `imul r32, r32, imm32` cannot hold a 64-bit constant, so no contiguous
  eight-byte copy of M exists anywhere in the image.  That is the whole reason a
  byte-pattern scan can only ever return coincidences: the four score-2 "candidates"
  it kept reporting at RVA 0x626C-0x6328 were inside the right function, scored on
  the wrong evidence.  Two lessons worth keeping: the constant is *split across
  instructions*, and the tool for finding it is a disassembler, not a memory scan.

  The function actually worth hooking is a string wrapper.  Following the callers of
  the 64-bit core finds one clean hasher:

      40bcd0:  83 ec 08 / 53 / 56  sub esp,0x8 / push ebx / push esi
      40bcd5:  8b 74 24 18          mov  esi,[esp+0x18]   <- the string, 2nd argument
      40bcd9:  8b ce / 8d 51 01     mov ecx,esi / lea edx,[ecx+1]
      40bce0:  8a 01 / 41 / 84 c0 / 75 f9
                                    byte-at-a-time strlen loop
      40bce7:  6a 00 / 6a 00       push 0 / push 0       <- seed = 0
      40bce9:  2b ca                sub  ecx,edx          <- length
      40bced:  51 / 56 / e8 1c a5 ff ff
                                    push ecx / push esi / call 0x406210
      40bcf4:  8b 5c 24 30 / 89 44 24 08 / 89 54 24 0c
                                    stores the 64-bit result (eax:edx)

  So `hash_string` at RVA 0x40BCD0 is __cdecl, takes the name as its *second* stack
  argument, and returns the hash in edx:eax - i.e. exactly MurmurHash64A(0, name,
  strlen(name)), the convention the catalogue has assumed all along.

  The core's own argument order is visible in its callers: 0x4042a3 and 0x40bcef
  both call it as `(data, len, 0, 0)`, while 0x43340f calls it as `(ptr, 4, 0x10, 0)`
  and then feeds the previous result (edx:eax) back in as the seed for the next
  field, a chained hash over struct members at +0x34/+0x2c.  So the core is
  `hash(const void* data, uint32_t len, seedLo, seedHi)`, and the string hasher is
  simply its seed-0, strlen-driven case.  Other callers, for the record: 0x404250
  (entered via 0x404240, which folds the result with `xor eax,edx`) builds a string
  into a 0x100-byte stack buffer through the `ds:0x746164` import and hashes it.

  Consequence for the capture project: [NameMining] now supports `HashNameAbi=3`
  (a two-argument __cdecl whose second argument is the string), and this build's
  settings are `HashNameAddress=0xBCD0`, `HashNameAbi=3`, `AutoHookHash=1`,
  `ScanHashFunction=0`.  The in-process image scan is retired: it could only have
  found the split immediates by luck.

  (Method note, and the third time this project has paid for the same lesson: a
  grep or byte scan over a *build artifact* answers only the question it was asked.
  The other two instances this session were `_names.txt` treated as a live resource
  list, and registry table names treated as resource names.  When a scan keeps
  returning "present but unnamed", read the consumer, not the bytes.)



60.16 THE SHADER FLAG WALL WAS A VOCABULARY GAP, NOT A RUNTIME GAP

  60.14 closed the shader question with two large sweeps - 40.7 M subset candidates
  carrying the `render_config` pass flags, then chain `mutate` to depth 4 - and
  concluded that the remaining 165 `shader_library` chains "use flags or techniques
  that appear nowhere in our vocabulary", leaving a frame capture as the only
  source.  That conclusion is wrong, and the correction is cheap: the words were
  already in sources we hold.  They had never been placed in the *flag position*.

  Every earlier shader run combined a CURATED flag list - the 100 flags mined from
  the named chains, the 13 render_config pass flags, or a keyword-filtered list from
  another BitSquid-family game.  A chain has only two kinds of position, so the
  probe that matters needs no curation at all:

      <known technique> : <any token>          80 x |tokens|
      <any token> : <known flag>               |tokens| x 100
      <any token>                              |tokens|

  `<tokens>` is pooled from everything on disk that could spell an engine word: the
  shipped Lua (`game-source`), the depot string harvest (`work/harvest2/raw.txt`),
  both runtime captures in `binaries/`, every token of every catalogue name, the
  strings of `gauntlet.exe` and `elias.dll`, and the Stingray documentation -
  348,633 tokens once hex runs, pure digits and 1-2 character fragments are dropped
  (requiring 3+ characters matters: it is what let `hdr` through in pass 2).  That
  is 63.1 M candidates, and all three shapes are expressible with the *pattern*
  engine, so the whole probe costs no candidate file at all:

      tools/hc.sh crack --gpu --targets data/vocab/unresolved_targets.txt \
          --list t=data/vocab/probe_techniques.txt \
          --list f=data/vocab/probe_tokens.txt --pattern '%t:%f'

  Result: **13 chains**, each re-hashed with the reference murmur before recording
  and none of which had a name in the catalogue.  Five passes, each recorded,
  merged and re-run with the flags it had just found:

      51.6 M   the three shapes above                          -> 9
      63.1 M   pass 2, relaxed filter (`hdr`, `hq`, `4x4`, …)   -> 1
     129.4 M   <the 7 yielding techniques>:<known flag>:<word>  -> 2
     138.4 M   pass 4, pass 1-3 flags fed back                  -> 1
     139.7 M   pass 5                                           -> 0  (fixpoint)

      global_lighting:debug_lights          global_lighting:sun_enabled
      copy:scale                            character:outline
      character:enemy                       lava_pool:vegetation_bending
      shadow_caster:instanced               tone_mapping:dust
      tone_mapping:hdr                      player_marker:deferred_decals_group_floor
      character:enemy:skinned_3weights      character:outline:skinned_3weights
      character:enemy:self_illumination

  Passes 3-5 compound, which is the whole point: `character:enemy` found in pass 1
  is a *known* word in pass 3, so the two-flag chains that contain it fall out
  without ever enumerating them.  The residue is also not one exotic token - three
  of the flags (`enemy`, `outline`, `instanced`) are *techniques* elsewhere, and
  `self_illumination` existed in our vocabulary only as `self_illumination_map`.
  It is ordinary engine vocabulary that a chain-derived flag list was incomplete
  for, which is exactly why curation kept failing.

  Effects: overlay 6,498 -> 6,511; catalogue unresolved 1,049 -> 1,036;
  `shader_library` targets 165 -> 152.

  What 60.14 got right: subsets over a *curated* pool are genuinely dead, and the
  1e13-1e18 blow-up for a wide pool at 6-9 flags is real, so the size model (body
  size -> technique + flag count) still matters for anything wider than one or two
  flags.  What it got wrong is the inference from "not in our flag list" to "not in
  our sources".  Reading the *position* the word has to occupy, rather than the
  list we happened to have, is the fourth instance this session of the same lesson.

60.17 THE MAP-SUFFIX SWEEP, AND THE NUMBER INSIDE THE SUFFIX PAIR

  60.16 closed the shader question on the *flag position*.  The same reasoning
  applied to textures closes the *suffix position*, and finds the last two.

  `tools/derive_tex_siblings.py` appends map suffixes to the geometry families
  (unit, decal, prop, block) and strips them from names that already carry one.
  It never asks a *texture* for its other maps, so a project where we hold
  `X_diff` is never asked for `X_norm` or `X_spec` - which is what "we may be
  missing diffs and norms" means.  Running the whole 77-suffix vocabulary over
  every name we hold, in the three forms that position allows,

      append   <name><suffix>        strip  <name minus suffix>
      swap     <stem><other suffix>

  is 593,939 candidates (6,781 names + 897 derived stems) and returns **0**.

  The wall is the shape, not the vocabulary, and the catalogue says so itself:
  `_01_diff` appears 44 times, `_01_norm` 43, `_02_diff` / `_02_norm` 5 each,
  `_03_diff`, `_03_norm`, `_01_spec` - a number *inside* the suffix pair.  No
  pass generated that: the number pass appends numbers at the end of a name
  (`moss_2`, then `moss_2_diff`), never before an existing map suffix.  Four
  pattern shapes, 88.2 M candidates, again with no candidate file:

      tools/hc.sh crack --gpu --targets data/vocab/unresolved_targets.txt \
          --list n=data/vocab/all_names.txt --list k=data/vocab/probe_numbers.txt \
          --list s=data/suffixes.txt \
          --pattern '%n_%k_%s' --pattern '%n%k_%s' \
          --pattern '%n_%s_%k' --pattern '%n_%k%s'

  Result: **2 names** -

      e31ea4f409715e97  props/d01_caves/caves_dragon_bones_01_diff
      0f1b125e15d37ed0  props/d01_caves/caves_dragon_bones_01_norm

  - both re-hashed against the reference murmur before recording, neither in the
  catalogue.  Folding them re-derives `caves_dragon_bones_01` as a stem, so the
  loop re-runs both sweeps; both return 0, a fixpoint in one round.

  The texture dimension, run to exhaustion, gave four sibling pairs:

      props/d01_lava/lava_wood_metal_set_01_{diff,norm}
      blocks/d01_lava/lava_waterfall_01_{diff,norm}
      props/d01_caves/caves_dragon_bones_01_{diff,norm}
      (plus the `achievement_icons/*_locked` pair, 60.16)

  Effects: overlay 6,520 -> 6,522; catalogue unresolved 1,027 -> 1,025; texture
  targets 317 -> 315.  Session total: 24 names (14 `shader_library`, 10 texture).

  This is the fifth instance of one lesson: a word or a shape has a *position*
  in the name, and curation inside that position keeps failing while the
  position itself is free.  Flag position (60.16), token position (60.14-60.16),
  number-before-suffix here.  `derive_tex_siblings.py` should take the
  `<stem>_<NN>_<suffix>` shape so `auto_run.sh` covers it without a bespoke run.


60.18 THE ICON AND RELIC FAMILIES: ONE NAME, AND A PATTERN TRAP

  The texture residue is not one family.  Reading each icon atlas as an *ordered*
  member list (an 8-byte hash per entry, resolved to a name or marked unnamed),
  and scanning the exported bodies for referenced hashes, gives the shape of what
  is left in the HUD / item space:

      equippables_icons/equipment   84 named, 16 unnamed   eq_<class>_<slot>_set<NN>
      hud/hud                       39 named,  6 unnamed   hud/<word>[_<word>]
      hud/action_icons              32 named,  3 unnamed   <hero>_<action|ultimate>_<w>
      equippables_icons/relics      10 named,  1 unnamed   equippables_icons/relic_<item>
      gameobjects/relics/vampire_heart_ring          -> the ring map is the last relic gap
                                                       (its material draws only it)

  One name came out, and it came out of the *document*:

      fb0dcb48ec930ca5   hud/action_icons/valkyrie_ultimate_vengeance

  61 identified this exact name - the sibling grammar's `..._ultimate_<ability>`
  with `<ability>` read from the talisman's flow event `ultimate_vengeance_windup`
  - and then left the hash unresolved.  The candidate was never run through the
  cracker.  Recording it is the whole gain.

  Everything else is a *closed* space, and all of it is exhausted:

      equippables_icons/eq_%c_%s_set%n, eq_%c_%s%n, eq_%c_%w%n       ~160 k
      hud/%h, %h_%g, %h_%n   (52 hud words, harvested from the atlas) ~2.7 k
      hud/action_icons/%c_{action,ultimate}_{%a, %n, %t}              ~140 M
      equippables_icons/{relic_%r, %r, relic_%r_tier%n}                ~1 k
      menu/shop/%t_%v, gameobjects/relics/%t_%v, potions/%t_%v     3 x 1.2e11
      gameobjects/relics/%t%u, effects/textures/%t%u                2 x 32 M

  Run on the GPU box (RTX 4060, 2.7-3.1 G/s on the token products; 3.7e11
  candidates total): the single name above.  So unlike the shader chains of 60.16,
  the icon residue is **not** a vocabulary gap - the unnamed atlas entries are not
  `<known member grammar>` with any engine token, with or without a map suffix, and
  not the `<stem>_<NN>_<suffix>` shape that named two textures in 60.17.  What is
  left there needs the art (61.2) or the shop/item tables, not more vocabulary.

  60.18.1 A TRAP IN `--pattern`: A REPEATED LETTER IS A BACKREFERENCE

  `%t_%t` over a 348,405-token list reports **348,405** candidates, not 1.2e11.
  Repeating a letter means *the same value*, not a second draw from the list.  That
  is deliberate and load-bearing - `modules/d01_%a/%b/%a_quilt_...` needs the
  environment repeated, and 13 shipped sweeps rely on it - but a product therefore
  needs its own letter:

      --list t=probe_tokens.txt --list v=probe_tokens.txt --pattern '%t_%v'

  which is 1.2e11 and runs at 3.1 G/s.  A "0 hits" must be read against the
  candidate count, not against the size of the vocabulary: the first draft of this
  hunt enumerated 348,405 candidates while claiming to test 1.2e11.

  60.18.2 `tools/pull-gpu.sh` GAVE `merge_matches.py` THE WRONG BASE

  It passed the git root; `merge_matches.py` wants the directory *containing*
  `GauntletGameDigging` (`Scripting/`).  The overlay merge died with
  FileNotFoundError *after* the union had already rewritten `matches.tsv`, so the
  recovered name reached the tsv and never reached the overlay - the failure is
  quiet unless the tool output is read.  Fixed to `"$REPO/Scripting"`; `*.sh` is
  gitignored, so this paragraph is the record rather than a diff.


60.19 THE ICON WORDS ARE GLUED SLUGS OF THE GAME'S OWN DISPLAY TEXT

  The 15 talisman item names (Thyra's Vow, Guardian Symbol, Mark of the Commander,
  Thor's Crummy Necklace, Bottomless Jug, The Prize, Merlin's Bookmark, Active
  Magicite, Mercury Vial, Questor's First Arrowhead, Enchanted Willow Branch,
  Dryad's Flute, Lilith's Heart, Dream Catcher, Reaper Charm) are the potion
  abilities - the localisation says so directly:

      "Each Hero has a Potion Ability that costs a Potion to use.  Unlock new
       Talismans to get new Potion Abilities."

  They are *item* names, and the icon is named after the *ability*, so testing the
  item slugs against the icon grammar (`hud/action_icons/<hero>_<ultimate|action>_<w>`)
  in every slug, glued, word and Lua-token form gives **0** - including every
  identifier that appears inside the talisman's own Lua (`potion_explosion`,
  `potion_throw`, `potion_projectile`, `wisps_cast`, `ultimate_wisp_projectile`,
  `ultimate_throw_windup`, `ultimate_wisps_windup`, ...).

  What does work is the *display* text.  Compare the two sides:

      strings: "More of Thor"   icon: hud/action_icons/warrior_ultimate_moreofthor
      strings: "Dryad's Song"   icon: hud/action_icons/elf_ultimate_dryadssong

  i.e. lowercase, strip apostrophes, glue the words.  So the vocabulary is the
  whole language file (`localization/game_strings.strings`, 91,936 null-separated
  values, a language-index + offset table with no text keys): take every 1-4 word
  n-gram that starts on a capitalised word, slug it glued and underscored (13,346
  forms), and sweep the icon / relic / hud / potion homes with it.

  Self-check: it *reproduces* 28 names we already held, across every sub-family -
  `hud/action_icons/*` ultimates and actions, `equippables_icons/relic_*`,
  `equippables_icons/equipment`, `hud/hud`, `hud/boss`, `hud/elements`, `hud/potion`,
  `hud/spells`.  Nothing else in this project has that property; it is why the rule
  is trusted.

  One name came out, and it closes the relic icons:

      f4870719d3c622cb   equippables_icons/relic_heart_of_vlad

  61.1 had argued this hash "is a relic icon" from the material that draws it and
  left it there; the display string is "Heart of Vlad".  Overlay 6,523 -> 6,524;
  unresolved 1,024 -> 1,023 (texture 314 -> 313).

  The two potion-ability icons that remain (`402c8d6e26caf96f`, the warrior's potion
  flask, and `965dc9490a86221e`, the elf's wisps) are *not* display slugs and not
  any Lua token of the two talismans that carry them, so unlike every other icon in
  the atlases they are not named after their own text.  Theirs is the art (61.2)
  and the UI code that resolves `icon = warrior_ultimate_01`, not more vocabulary.

  Machinery: `sweeps/localization_hunt.sh` (tiers: homes, homes + map suffix,
  display slugs x the 348 k-token pool in both orders) and
  `sweeps/talisman_hunt.sh` (the item names in every form); `data/vocab/loc_slugs.txt`
  is generated from the export by `tools/make_loc_slugs.py`.


60.20 THE TWO ICONS THAT BREAK THE RULE (AND THEIR ABILITIES ARE KNOWN)

  Two entries in `hud/action_icons` remain, and they are the only ones in any of the
  icon atlases that are *not* named after their own display text:

      402c8d6e26caf96f   warrior  "Improvise"        - the grenade-toss potion
      965dc9490a86221e   elf      "Spirit Guardians" - the will-o-wisp potion

  Both were read off the game rather than derived, and both match 61.2's art exactly:
  the warrior's is a hand gripping a blue potion flask, the elf's is green swirling
  wisp energy.  28 of the 30 atlas entries that were ever unnamed *do* reproduce from
  a display-string slug (60.19) - including the third, `valkyrie_ultimate_vengeance`,
  and the relic icon `equippables_icons/relic_heart_of_vlad`.  These two do not.

  Everything the names and their descriptions allow is negative:

    * the display words and the *new* words those descriptions give (`grenade`,
      `toss`, `willowisp`, `willo_wisp`, `guardians`, `spirit`, ...) in every slug /
      glued / underscored / hyphen / case form (incl. `IMPROVISE`, `improvise!`), in
      every `{hero}_{action,ultimate,ability,special,potion,talisman,ability_icon,
      icon,spell,skill,throw}_{word}[_01|01|_1|_icon|_large|_small]` position and in
      every icon / relic / hud / potion / effects home - ~20 k forms, 0;
    * the same words with a 350,242-word vocabulary on **either side** (the
      348,405-token engine pool *unioned with the 7,635 words of the display text* -
      necessary, because a word like `guardians` is in no Lua at all) in
      `hud/action_icons/`, `effects/textures/`, `gameobjects/potions/`,
      `equippables_icons/` and `hud/` - ~1.1e9 candidates, 0;
    * the glued two-token shape the resolved ones actually use (`moreofthor`,
      `dryadssong`, `hypersniper`) after `_ultimate_` and after `_potion_` - 2 x
      2.5e11 candidates, 0.

  Two structural negatives:

    * `hud/action_icons/warrior_ultimate_improvise` hashes to `a53249b9dd323d33`,
      which is in **neither** the catalogue **nor** the atlas.  So these two are not
      named after their ability at all: the atlas *region* is presumably the id
      (`warrior_ultimate_01`, `elf_ultimate_03` - exactly what the item Lua carries)
      while the *texture* the artist saved carries some other name.
    * both hashes **do** exist in the pre-release builds (2015-01-21, 2015-08-11), so
      the textures shipped then and their names were in use - but that build's
      20,095,020-string harvest (`_names.txt`) holds no string hashing to either of
      them, so the old name is not recoverable that way either.

  The words exist in exactly one source: the localisation.  Not in `gauntlet.exe`,
  not in `elias.dll` (both checked, negative), not in any Lua, not in the depot
  harvest.  So no vocabulary route reaches these two; what is left is the UI code
  that resolves `icon = warrior_ultimate_01` into an atlas entry, or the art (61.2).

  60.20.1 TWO THINGS `crack --candidates` DOES THAT MISLEAD BY OMISSION

    * **It lowercases every candidate.**  A target for `A--` matches the candidate
      written `A--` and is reported as `a--`; that is the project's convention, but a
      candidate file of uppercase spellings silently tests their lowercase forms.
    * **It skips names already in `matches.tsv`** - correctly, since they are not
      news - so `total cands : 0` with `new matches : 0` is *normal* for a file whose
      best candidates we already hold.  Re-testing a name we have therefore reports
      nothing at all, which reads like the file was never opened; use an empty
      `--matches` file for a control.

    Both bit during this hunt, and both are why the old-build harvest looked skipped
    when it had in fact been tested in 0.0 s.


61. THE `hud/action_icons` SIBLING TABLE, AND THE LAST TWO "POTION" ICONS

  `hud/action_icons` is not itself an icon: it is the *material* for the HUD
  action-button art, and its body lists the icon textures it draws as one 52-byte
  entry each (8-byte texture hash + flags + an autogenerated u64 id, 32-byte
  header, then N entries).  It holds **35** entries:

      32 named   <hero>_<action|ultimate>_<ability>
                 warrior_action_{spin,groundslam,demoncleave,hacknslash}
                 necromagus_action_{archers,dominate,ghosts,soldiers}
                 valkyrie_action_{crescentsweep,intercept,linebreaker,shieldthrow}
                 elf_action_{explosive,root,mortar,shadow}_{bomb,shot}
                 <hero>_ultimate_{moreofthor,hunger,arcaneblast,polymorph,barrier,
                                  hypersniper,dryadssong,aegis,valkyries,
                                  darkhunger,souleater,resurrect,vengeance}
       3 unresolved, of which one (fb0dcb48ec930ca5) is `valkyrie_ultimate_vengeance`
                 -> **2 remain**.

61.1 WHAT THE SIBLING TABLE IS FOR

  The same method that named the four colosseum grades (§ the `+` suffix): find
  the material that draws the resource, read its sibling list, and let the named
  neighbours show the grammar.  Here the grammar is `<hero>_<action|ultimate>_
  <ability>` and the ability word is the talisman's own flow event, not a
  resource token - `ultimate_vengeance_windup` gives `..._ultimate_vengeance`,
  `ultimate_aegis_execute` gives `..._ultimate_aegis`, `ultimate_dryadssong_windup`
  gives `..._ultimate_dryadssong`, and the wizard's `ultimate_barrier_windup`
  gives `wizard_ultimate_barrier` (the one whose word is a plain English noun).

  `equippables_icons/shop_action_icons` is the shop's copy of the same list and
  references the same hashes, plus one more unresolved, `f4870719d3c622cb`, which
  is **not** an action icon at all - `equippables_icons/relics` and
  `hud/relic_icons` both draw it, so it is a relic icon (`equippables_icons/
  relic_*`).  Only the two below are under `hud/action_icons/`.

61.2 THE TWO THAT REMAIN - IDENTIFIED VISUALLY, NOT BY NAME

      402c8d6e26caf96f   <- hand gripping a blue potion flask, red/orange ground
      965dc9490a86221e   <- green swirling wisp energy with orbiting motes

  An icon's background colour is its hero's: the warrior icons sit on red/orange,
  the elf's on green (checked against `warrior_action_spin`, `warrior_ultimate_
  {moreofthor,hunger}` vs `elf_ultimate_{dryadssong,hypersniper}`).  So the two
  unresolved are the **warrior's t01 ultimate** (talisman01, the
  `potion_throw`/`potion_explosion` ability, `unit_path = potion_projectile`) and
  the **elf's t03 ultimate** (talisman03, the `wisps_cast` ability,
  `unit_path = ultimate_wisp_projectile`).  Both are store-able (43836 bytes,
  256x256 DDS): extract the body, write it as `.dds` and PIL opens it - the art is
  the identification when the name will not come.

61.3 THE NAMES: EVERY CONVENTIONAL SWEEP IS EXHAUSTED

  Nothing in the corpus produces either name.  Tried, all 0:

    * `<hero>_{action,ultimate,ability,special}_{w}` and `<hero>_{w}` over the
      union of every token we hold (273,778 single tokens: loc words, Lua tokens,
      bundle-body harvest, depot harvest, names.txt);
    * two-token forms `<hero>_{,action,ultimate}_{w1}_{w2}` over the localisation
      vocabulary (5979 words) and `<{w1}_{w2}>` with no hero (893 M candidates);
    * **glued** two-word names - the form the recovered ones actually use
      (`dryadssong`, `moreofthor`, `darkhunger`, `souleater`, `arcaneblast`,
      `hypersniper`) - over 15,972,706 glued pairs x hero grammar (399 M);
    * a wide middle slot (`power/skill/spell/relic/throw/...` x 46) x the 273 k
      tokens, and `..._potion` / `..._wisp` suffixed forms.

  The ability words are hand-authored game text, and for these two they are
  simply not in any source we hold - not the shipped Lua, not the localisation,
  not the bundles or the depot harvest.  This is the same wall §57.4 hit
  (class-specific ability words that were never written into a resource), and the
  same conclusion §60.13 reached: the residual must come from grammar+vocabulary
  or not at all.  The two hashes and their identity are recorded here so the art
  at least pins what they *are* while the words stay open.


61.4 OPENING THE UNRESOLVED TEXTURES - THE ART IS THE IDENTIFICATION

  The export decodes the unresolved textures to PNG as well
  (`view/png/_unresolved/texture/<hash>.png`) and they are *small* - the 1.4 MB
  1024 level map is a ~1.1 MB PNG.  There is no OCR to do: this is game art, not
  documents, and what "text" is in it is a rock, a mask colour or a skull.  What
  the pixels give is *identity*, which is what the two potion icons needed in 61.2
  and what the level-material gaps need here.  `tools/montage_png.py` turns any PNG
  set into a labelled contact sheet (`--from-dir`, `--from-list`, `--size WxH`,
  `--checker`, `--label-mode hash|stem|path`), so a whole group is *looked at* in
  one image instead of one file at a time.  Sorted by size, the 314 unresolved are:

      256x256 x69   128x128 x33   512x512 x52   1024x1024 x20   640x272 x9
      1920x1080/880 x4 (title art)  + ~80 one-off UI sizes (580x320, 1200x480, ...)

  Read off the sheets:

    * 1024x1024 with mean RGB ~ (128,128,0) - **normal maps** (BC5: R and G only,
      B = 0), i.e. the missing `_norm` partners of level materials: moss, sand
      ripples, cobbles, cut stone, rubble, a rosette/pentagram inlay floor, arches.
    * 512x512 RGB - the same thing, all normals, and legible as props: a column
      arcade, a tiled floor medallion, cobble walls, a ruined beam, scattered
      bones, a boss corbel, a fabric banner with fringe.
    * 1024x2048 / 512x1024 flat 0x00FF00 + 0x00FFFF - **cloth masks**.  Pixels
      confirm the type: the *named* capes (`equipment/capes/cape_s0X_r0Y`) have the
      same signature (mean ~ (50,255,200), 40-80 % pure cyan/green), so the cape
      textures are shader/cloth masks, not painted fabric.
    * 1920x880 - a mountain with a **skull carved into the peak**: the title screen.
    * 256x256 transparent - rubble/dirt piles, gold UI frames and rings, a red
      cave-painting wall, hanging fur/banner strips, magenta dev swatches.

  `tools/twin_by_image.py` is the machine version of this pass: it L1-compares each
  unresolved PNG against every *named* PNG of the same dimensions (48x48 RGB+A) and
  prints the closest named twins - `props/d01_crypt/cloth_01_norm` (0.010) for a
  1024 cloth normal, `blocks/d01_crypt/crypt_floor_01_norm` (0.026), `equipment/
  capes/cape_s05_r01` for the cloth masks.  Use it to choose the family to guess
  in; do not read it as a name (the pixel-nearest named texture is normally a
  different *map* of the same material).

61.5 WHAT THE REFERENCES SAY THOSE ARE

  `work/refs_texture.json` names the units that draw the unresolved bodies, and for
  the striking ones the identity is immediate:

      2b1bdea63cb3e2f8 <- props/menu/titlescreen_background    (the skull mountain)
      0451f9d391a4ce38 <- props/d01_caves/ground_ornament_02   (a stone ornament)
      006092561807ee0b, 02645408743ec88f, c01c37816b794f76
                       <- equipment/{elf,necromagus,valkyrie,warrior,wizard}/cape_s03

  The last three are the cape tree's cleanest hole: `equipment/capes/` holds
  `cape_s0{1,2,4,5,6}` x `r01..r03` and **no s03 at all**, and exactly three
  unresolved textures (one 1024x2048, two 512x1024) are drawn by `cape_s03` in all
  five classes.  Their names are not any `cape_s03*` form, though: 421,578
  candidates over 6 dirs x 3 bases x 46 style spellings x 16 rarity spellings x 9
  suffixes -> 0.

61.6 THE CAPE SUB-FAMILIES: FOUR RECOVERED, AND WHY THE TOKEN LIST WINS

  Swapping a *token* into one slot of a name we hold (Rust `mutate`, `--tokens`)
  recovered names no sibling walk could reach, because the words are ones no
  sibling sweep had:

      66b02cb8e2a7ac88  equipment/capes/cape_collar_r01
      18fc99eba2227ec7  equipment/capes/cape_collar_r02
      a6c107981bb4a3ce  equipment/capes/cape_collar_r03
      f1b2a4d3b95faacc  props/d01_caves/caves_skullpiles_01_diff

  (`cape_collar_r0X` out of `tokens_big.txt`, `caves_skullpiles_01_diff` out of
  `big_dict.txt`.)  So the cape tree is `cape_<part>_r<nn>` *and* `cape_s<nn>_r<nn>`,
  and a `caves_*` family exists next to the `caverock_*`/`cavestruct_*` ones.  This
  is the general lesson for the residual: the reach comes from the *token list*, not
  from the pattern.  `cape_collar_r0X` is 3 names off a 100k-word list dropped into
  the position where a sibling sweep only had `s01..s06`.

  Note for the next run: the mode's summary line lied.  That run printed
  `new matches : 0` and then `matches file : matches.tsv (5928 total)` - the hits
  are real (their hashes verify and they are absent from the overlay), so trust the
  file total, not the per-run counter.

61.7 EVERYTHING ELSE TRIED FOR THESE 309 (all 0)

  New tools, all hash-verified, all negative:

    * `tools/mutate_named_textures.py` - mutate *every* named texture name (stem
      truncation x 120 suffixes x numbers x dir moves x dir grammar): 2,594,864.
    * level dirs (16) x `loc_slugs.txt` (13,346 display-text slugs) x level
      suffixes x family/number shapes, on the GPU: **812,718,016 candidates in
      1.06 s**, 0.
    * `tools/gen_texture_candidates.py` re-run on the reference map: 1,387,654, 0.
    * Rust `mutate` over the 1,483 named texture names x `tex_suffixes` (92):
      `mut_tokens` (1,391) 0, `tokens_big` (80,000) 0 **plus the three cape_collar
      names**, `bundle_words` (108,880) and the rest of the big lists running.
    * targeted guesses with `tools/check_names.py` (stdin -> hits in milliseconds,
      `--write` appends): `titlescreen_background*`, `ground_ornament_0X*`,
      per-class `cape_s03*`, the whole cape style/rarity space -> 0.

  So the residual textures are not siblings of anything we hold, not named after
  the unit that draws them, and not the product of the level grammar with either
  our stems or the game's display language.  Like 61.3 the words are simply not in
  the pattern vocabularies - but they *are* in the token lists: one word in one
  slot is what `cape_collar` was, and every extra token list is another 100k shots
  at the same slot.


61.8 WHAT THE TOKEN ENGINE UNLOCKED: 30 NAMES, AND EVERY ONE OPENS A SUBTREE

  `hashcracker mutate` with a big mined token list is the first method in this
  project that keeps paying after every pattern sweep has flat-lined.  Thirty names
  in one session, and they are not siblings of each other - each opens a family:

    blocks/d01_caves/swamp_broken_wall_2c_{hm,ml,m,l}_{diff,norm}    (8) - the walls
    blocks/d01_caves/cavestruct_floor_track_{mark_diff,mark_norm,turn}
    blocks/d01_hub_room/tutorial_platform_{diff,norm}
    blocks/d01_caves/caverock_stalagmites_01b_diff
    blocks/d01_lava/lavastruct_borders_01b_diff
    props/d01_caves/caves_dragon_bones_01b_diff
    props/d01_caves/caves_skullpiles_01_{diff,norm}
    equipment/capes/cape_collar_r01..r03
    menu/level_select/map_rock_l01..l06        (l = left; r01..r06 were already held)
    effects/textures/additive_{flare_01,lightning_arc}
    characters/avatar_valkyrie/animations/character_select_{turnleft,turnright}
    characters/avatar_warrior/animations/character_select_pos02_{turnleft,turnright}
    characters/avatar_necromagus/abilities/ultimate_remnant_illu
    characters/mummy_bloated/mummy_bloated_illu

  Sub-lessons, in order of how much they change the next run:

  * The residual is NOT only textures.  The 1019 missing hashes are 326 animation,
    309 texture, 151 shader_library, 107 material, 91 particles, 47 unit, 37
    timpani_bank, 36 shader, 10 level, 2 package, 1 flow.  `mutate` does not care
    what a name belongs to: a seed contributes a directory and a leaf token list, so
    an animation name is as good a seed as a texture, and `character_select_pos02_
    turnleft` came out of exactly that.  `tools/named_textures.py --all` feeds all
    6812 known names for this reason; the texture-only list is 1483.
  * `_illu` is a real map suffix (illumination) and `_01b` a real letter-variant slot
    (`01a/01b/01c`), used by blocks, props and characters alike.  Neither is
    reachable by number substitution: `mutate` replaces a whole digit token, so
    `_01` -> `_07` but never `_01b`, and a token like `l01` would lose its letter.
    `tools/letter_variants.py` covers the slot explicitly: 479,681 candidates over
    every name we hold.  It reproduced the six `map_rock_lXX` and both `_01b` names
    independently - grammar confirmed - and added 0, because the engine had beaten it
    by minutes.  That is the honest reading of the test: it is not that the slot is
    unreachable, it is that the token lists already reach it.
  * Two directories appeared that no list contained before: `effects/textures/`
    (`additive_*`, `alpha_*`, `atlas_*`, `normal_*` families) and
    `menu/level_select/` (`map_rock_l/r01..06`, `level_button_*`, `flow_background_*`).
  * `tools/wallgen.py` tested the "walls and bricks" reading of the normals head-on:
    level dirs x ~100 families (including swampstruct/masonry/cobble/stone ones no
    held name uses) x the 352 mined parts x map suffixes, flat and under `walls/` -
    3,639,712 candidates, 0.  The walls that were actually missing are not
    `<family>_wall_<n>`, they are `swamp_broken_wall_2c_<piece>`: the answer was in
    the token list again.
  * `tools/neighbour_mutate.py` is the CPU complement to the GPU engine: inside each
    directory, replace one leaf token with any token that already lives in the same
    directory (the game's own vocabulary for that folder - `turnleft`/`pos02` in
    `characters/*/animations/`, `windup`/`start` in an attack set, `_2c`/`_low` in a
    block set), plus append/prepend one.  16,641,631 candidates over 372 directories
    in 10 s, single-threaded, no GPU, no queue behind the lock.
  * `tools/token_mutate_loop.sh` is the loop that keeps this going: it rebuilds the
    seed list from matches.tsv every round (`mutate` is self-feeding - a name found
    in round N is a seed in round N+1, with its directory), walks the token lists in
    order of what has paid, and stops at a fixpoint.  `sweeps/level_slug_hunt.sh`
    covers the level-material half.

  Scoreboard for the session: matches.tsv 5925 -> 5960, overlay 6528 -> 6563.



61.9 THE RESIDUAL IS 11 TYPES, NOT ONE: PER-TYPE VOCABULARIES

  `hashcracker mutate` had been fed `tex_suffixes.txt` (`_diff`/`_norm`/`_spec`) whatever
  the target was, which is noise in an animation name.  The 978 hashes still missing are

      animation 326   texture 309   shader_library 151   material 107   particles 91
      unit 46   timpani_bank 37   shader 36   level 10   package 2   flow 1

  so `tools/mine_type_tokens.py` now mines three files per type out of the names we
  hold for that type - `seeds.txt` (the `--names` input), `suffixes.txt` (tokens that
  *end* a held name of that type, which for animation is `windup`, `turnleft`,
  `offset_02`, `exit`) and `tokens.txt` (every leaf token) - and
  `sweeps/type_battery.sh` spends them in rounds.  Round A (each type on its own
  vocabulary alone, seconds per type) immediately found six:

      characters/avatar_valkyrie/animations/move_jog_break_left
      gameobjects/traps/trap_floor_blades_normal
      decal_projector:deferred_decals_group_floor:deferred_decals_group_level:diffuse_map:normal_aware
      decal_projector:deferred_decals_group_floor:...:deferred_decals_group_prop:diffuse_map:normal_aware

  Two lessons from that, both about *grouping* rather than vocabulary:

  * The unit is the missing coordinate.  The unresolved animations sit in
    `resource_packages/d01_caves` (83), `d01_lava` (79), **`morak` (77)**, `d01_crypt`
    (66), `d01_arcade` (61) and the hero bases - i.e. in units, and the package name is
    how you find out which.  `neighbour_mutate.py` pools tokens by *directory*, so a
    unit's `abilities/` and `gib/` words could never reach its `animations/`; with
    `--group-by 2` (pool by `characters/<unit>/`) the same-size space yielded
    `move_jog_look_left_back` and `move_jog_look_right_back`, which the per-directory
    pass had missed.  Same words, different grouping, different answer.
  * Insertion, not replacement.  `move_jog_left` -> `move_jog_look_left` needs a token
    *inserted* mid-name; a swap-only pass cannot spell it.  `neighbour_mutate.py` now
    tries every insertion point, and `move_jog_{look,break}_{left,right}[_back]` came
    out of it.  (`hashcracker mutate` reaches these only through its append/prepend
    forms, which is why the animation family lagged the texture families.)

  Negative results, with their sizes, all hash-verified:

    * `tools/enumerate_spaces.py` - the four *parseable* spaces, 568,388 candidates:
      quilts (`modules/<mod>/quilts/<cat>/<lvl>_quilt_<shape>__<grid>__<NN>[_<sfx>]`,
      including synthesized `lava_quilt_*` heads, since we only held caves and crypt),
      `audio/<group>` (every unit x every audio prefix), `flow/status_effect_*` (45
      status words x numbers) and `shaders/<name>` (every token as a shader name plus
      every held shader with a variant suffix, because 23 of the 36 missing `shader`
      rows are in `resource_packages/menu`) - **0**.
    * cross-unit animation token cross (per-directory pooling): 4,348,175 - 0.  The
      same space *grouped by unit* then found 4, which is the point above.
    * `tools/enumerate_chains.py` - a shader chain is `technique:<flag:flag:...>` with
      the flags sorted, so the space is technique x sorted subsets: 43 techniques x
      subsets <= 3 of 109 flags = 9,284,990 candidates, enumerated once instead of
      once per seed (which is what `mutate --chain-depth` does).  It paid: after
      44,955,607 candidates it returned

          character:enemy:silhouette:silhouette_decals_group_level

      - a *technique we did not hold* (`character`), with two flags (`enemy`,
      `silhouette`) that no held chain name contains.  Both came from the pool the
      tool mixes in (the `shader_library_group` / `render_config` tokens), which is
      the lesson: for chains the pool is the reach, exactly as it is for textures.
      `--wide` widens it further with every flag-shaped token in the corpus (53
      techniques x 253 flags, depth 3 = 143,060,674).

61.10 ANIMATION, SECOND PASS: BODY PARTS, CHARSELECT, UNIT VARIANTS

  The animation names that came out of these passes:

      characters/avatar_elf/animations/talisman_03_lowerbody
      characters/avatar_necromagus/animation/charselect_01_directional_{up,down,left,right}
      characters/avatar_valkyrie/animations/move_jog_{look,break}_{left,right}[_back]
      characters/avatar_valkyrie/animations/move_jog_look_left_back

  Which adds three axes to the animation grammar on top of 61.9:

    * a *body part* axis (`_lowerbody`/`_upperbody`, and the same slot in `_lowerbody`
      for every ability);
    * a `charselect_<NN>_<action>_<direction>` family (the character-select screen),
      spelled with `animation/` not `animations/` for the necromagus;
    * unit *variants* as separate units (`boss_morak_sword`, `avatar_necromagus_
      summoned_archer`), each with its own full animation set.

61.11 THE SHIPPED LUA NAMES RESOURCES IT DOES NOT SPELL OUT (*)

  `lua/generated/` had never been read as a *name list*, and it is one: the tree
  ships `unit_paths.lua` (245 literal paths), `settings_paths.lua` (289),
  `unit_path_to_components.lua` (1813), `unit_path_to_network_object.lua` (1174),
  `characters_unit_path.lua` (63), `animation_events.lua` (493 event names) and
  `flow_events.lua` (1090).  They are generated *from the data*, so they are
  authoritative - but every one of those paths is already a name we hold (0
  unknown, the same result as 60.13 for the source tree itself).

  The reach is in the *join*, because the game writes unit-relative leaves:
  `preview_hero.lua` says `Unit.animation_event(self.unit, "charselect_turn")`, not
  `characters/avatar_valkyrie/animations/charselect_turn`.  `mine_lua_expand.py`
  therefore hashes `<directory> + <leaf>` for every directory in the corpus (plus
  every directory the Lua spells out, plus `characters/<unit>/{animations,animation}/`
  for each unit in `characters_unit_path.lua`) x every leaf the Lua contains
  (quoted strings **and bare table keys** - `special_root_bomb = {` is how the
  config tables name things, and quoting-only mining misses them).

  Result, 27,057,030 candidates: 13 hits, and they are a whole family -

      resource_packages/elias/cave_adventure     .../lava_boss
      resource_packages/elias/cave_arena         .../lava_labyrinth
      resource_packages/elias/cave_boss          resource_packages/elias/hubroom
      resource_packages/elias/crypt_{adventure,arena,boss,labyrinth}

  i.e. `<env>_{adventure,arena,boss,labyrinth}` for cave/crypt/lava.  Widening the
  leaf pool with bare keys and the directory pool with Lua dirs (25,338,138 more)
  adds nothing further: the elias packages were the whole yield of this vein.

  Dead ends recorded so they are not re-run:

    * every quoted literal of all 927 `.lua` files, verbatim (11,799 literals) - 0.
    * `charselect_01_directional_<dir>` / `charselect_<NN>_<action>_<dir>` /
      `character_select_*` / `charselect_turn[_<dir>]` for **every** unit in the
      generated unit lists (89 units) x unit variants x `{animations,animation,
      abilities,ability}/` - 1,416,168 candidates, 0.  The heroes' select-screen
      animations are therefore named with words that are *not* in the shipped text
